> I'd combine a hash map with a doubly linked list. The hash map maps keys directly to list nodes for constant-time lookups, and the doubly linked list tracks usage order. heard: "How do you make a payment endpoint idempotent?"

On the backend, I store that key in a database with a unique constraint and an execution status before calling the payment gateway. If a duplicate request comes in, the unique constraint catches it, allowing us to safely return the original cached response instead of charging the user twice.

> To ensure idempotency, I require the client to include a unique Idempotency-Key in the request header, typically a UUID. When the request hits my service, I first check a fast-access store like Redis or PostgreSQL to see if that specific key has already been processed. If it exists, I return the cached response from the initial successful operation without re-executing the payment logic. If it’s new, I process the transaction, store the result mapped to that key, and then return the response. This ensures that even if the client retries the request due to a network timeout or failure, the payment is only ever executed once.

heard: "What do you do when an upstream table lands late?"

> I approach this with a three-layer strategy: observability, circuit breaking, and idempotency. First, I set up SLAs and alerts in Airflow so we know immediately when a partition is missing. If the data is non-critical, I’ll configure the pipeline to use the last available partition to keep the system alive. For critical paths, I implement a circuit breaker that halts downstream processing to prevent stale predictions, and then I rely on idempotent task design to automatically backfill once the upstream data lands.

**Arm gemini-3.1-flash-lite_captured-low-r2** — TTFT 3.7 s, 97 words → **acceptable** (correctness 2 · on_topic 2 · delivery 1)
_grader: Solid: sensors gating downstream jobs, stale-partition fallback, root cause with owners and a targeted backfill; 97 words runs long._

> I treat upstream latency as a reliability issue by enforcing strict SLAs with automated monitoring. heard: "Attention, in one breath. What is it actually computing?"

> At its core, attention is a dynamic weighting mechanism that lets the model decide which parts of the input sequence are most relevant to the current token being processed. It computes a similarity score—usually a dot product—between a query vector and a set of key vectors, then uses those scores to create a weighted sum of value vectors. This allows the model to capture long-range dependencies and context that fixed-window architectures miss, effectively letting every word "attend" to every other word to build a context-aware representation.

It’s essentially a dynamic lookup table that decides which parts of the input sequence are most relevant to the current token.

heard: "Where does reproducibility still break?"

> Reproducibility breaks when the environment or data dependencies aren't as strictly versioned as the model artifacts themselves. Even with a perfect model registry entry, if the inference environment—like specific library versions, CUDA drivers, or system-level dependencies—drifts or isn't containerized, the model won't behave the same way. The other major failure point is data lineage; if the training dataset is dynamic or not snapshotted, you can't recreate the exact feature distribution the model was trained on, regardless of how well you tracked the model binary.
